### Page 65

Summary
Chapter 3
Outlook
• The outlook for inflation has been revised materially higher, especially in the earlier
part of the forecast period; the forecasts assume that the cash rate follows the
market path. A number of upside risks flagged in the August Statement have since been
incorporated into the central inflation forecast. Fuel and other commodity prices affected
by the ongoing Middle East conflict are expected to contribute more to underlying and

headline inflation than previously expected. Capacity constraints are also judged to have
been somewhat greater throughout this year than we thought in August and inflation
expectations have increased, adding to inflationary pressures. Underlying inflation is now
expected to remain above 3 per cent until late 2027 and to reach 21⁄2 per cent
around mid-2028.
• Headline inflation is expected to remain above 3 per cent until late 2027, around
three quarters longer than in the August forecast. Headline inflation is expected to

peak at 4.5 per cent in the December 2026 quarter and reach the midpoint of the
2–3 per cent inflation target a bit later than was expected in August. The decline in
inflation is expected to reflect conflict-related cost pressures easing and spare capacity
emerging in the labour market and economy more broadly. Risks to the domestic inflation
outlook are more balanced than they were in the August Statement, but still skewed to the
upside. This reflects the incorporation in the central inflation forecast of some of the risks

identified in August. However, inflation could be higher if global and domestic cost
pressures are more persistent than assumed, or if inflation expectations rise further or exert
greater influence on wage- and price-setting.
• The outlook for growth in Australia’s major trading partners has been revised
higher, owing to a stronger outlook for AI-related activity and, more broadly,
resilient demand. Growth is still expected to moderate from its current strong pace over

the next couple of years, as the boost from AI-related activity slows and growth in China
continues to ease. The risks to the outlook are relatively balanced. AI-related activity could
continue to be stronger than expected, while ongoing higher energy prices could weigh
on growth by more than anticipated. The rise in global interest rates presents risks on both
sides of the outlook: it may signal stronger demand and investment than currently
expected, but may also result in tighter financial conditions than assumed.

• The global inflation outlook has also been revised higher, reflecting renewed
pressure from energy and other commodity prices, resilient demand and rapid
growth in AI-related prices. The oil price assumption underpinning the forecasts is much
higher than in August, reflecting the re-escalation of tensions in the Middle East in recent
months. Consensus forecasters expect headline inflation to remain above central bank
targets into 2027 in many advanced economies as a result of higher energy costs. More

broadly, increases in a range of commodity prices and strong AI-related demand are
driving up input costs, which are expected to be passed through to inflation to some
degree. The risks to global inflation are judged to remain skewed to the upside.
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 62

### Page 66

• Australian GDP growth is expected to remain below estimates of potential growth
over coming years. Based on financial market pricing, the forecasts assume the cash rate
will increase by around 35 basis points by mid-2027; this cash rate path is up to 50 basis
points higher than was assumed in the August forecasts. Compared with August, the
outlook for GDP growth over the year ahead has been revised higher, driven by a stronger
outlook for data centre investment. From mid-2027, however, forecast growth has been

revised lower because of the higher assumed cash rate path and a weaker outlook for the
housing market. Higher-than-expected global energy prices is also expected to weigh a
little on activity. Overall, the forecast level of GDP at end-2028 is broadly unchanged from
the August forecasts. The risks to domestic growth appear to be broadly balanced. There
are upside risks to global and domestic economic activity from AI-related demand, but
economic activity could be weaker if the Middle East conflict and the decline in housing

prices affect the economy by more than assumed in the central forecast.
• Labour market conditions are expected to ease further, consistent with
below-potential GDP growth. The unemployment rate is forecast to increase to
5.1 per cent by end-2028. This is around 1⁄4 percentage points higher than previously
expected, largely reflecting the higher starting point for the unemployment rate and an
expectation that more people will remain in or join the labour force.

• Despite this, we assess that capacity constraints in the economy and labour market
have been a little tighter than previously judged, contributing more to wage and
inflationary pressures (see Chapter 2: Economic Conditions). From this starting point, we
judge that aggregate demand and potential supply in the economy are likely to reach
balance next year (slightly later than previously thought), before spare capacity emerges. It
is possible we have misjudged the supply capacity of the economy and labour market.

The central forecast incorporates many judgements,
such as the choice of models used and whether to
deviate from the models given the signal from recent
data or information from liaison. These judgements are
extensively considered and debated during the
construction of the forecast. The three most important
judgements for our current assessment of the economic
outlook are discussed below.
The annual review of the staff’s economic forecasts will
be discussed at the December Board meeting (and the

results will be published following the meeting). The
review will assess what we have learned about the
economy, how the judgements that underpin the
forecasts have fared and how the risks around the
outlook have evolved.
Key judgement #1 – Global oil and
refined product prices decline
gradually but remain above
pre-conflict levels.
The assumption that global oil and related prices decline
gradually but remain above levels prior to the Middle
East conflict underpins the central forecasts (Graph 3.1).

The assumed oil price in the December quarter is
35 per cent higher than assumed in August, following a
re-escalation of the conflict in recent months. From this
higher starting point, oil prices are assumed to be
11 per cent higher by end-2028 than assumed in August.
This assumed decline is consistent with energy markets
continuing to adjust to the conflict and/or progress
towards resolution of the conflict, and is within the
range of the latest Consensus forecasts. The spreads

between prices for refined fuels and Brent crude oil,
which have widened since the start of the conflict, are
assumed to return to their historical averages by
mid-2027 – around two quarters later than assumed in
the August Statement. Oil and refined product prices
could rise further or remain elevated for longer than
assumed, which would drive up global and domestic
inflation and could also weigh on activity (see Key risk #1
and Key risk #3).

Key judgement #2 – Restrictive

financial conditions will contribute to
the emergence of some spare
capacity in the economy and the
labour market, helping reduce
inflationary pressures.
The forecasts are conditioned on a market path for the
cash rate that increases by 35 basis points by mid-2027,
before declining a little to stabilise at 4.8 per cent in
2028. This is up to 50 basis points higher than the path
that underpinned the August forecasts. Conditioned on
that path, the forecast is for a period of below-potential

growth that is sufficient to generate some spare capacity
in the economy and help return inflation to target. In
other words, the assumed path for policy is judged to
be restrictive.
One way to cross-check the plausibility of this
judgement is to compare the market path with current
estimates of the neutral cash rate derived from models
and market economists. The assumed cash rate path lies
above the top of the range of these estimates (see
Chapter 1: Financial Conditions). These estimates are

subject to considerable uncertainty, however, and it is
common for them to be revised as more information
becomes available. A key question is how to interpret
3.1 Key judgements in the forecast
Chapter 3  |  OutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlook
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 64

### Page 68

the recent material increase in longer-term real yields
(see Box A: How Does an Increase in Longer-term Real
Yields Affect Financial Conditions? and Box B:
Developments in the Neutral Rate of Interest). To the
extent that this reflects higher neutral policy rates
globally (and for Australia), the assumed cash rate path
would, by itself, be less restrictive and could weigh less
on activity than projected. On the other hand, if the
recent rise in global yields primarily reflects higher term

premia, that would imply tighter financial conditions
than assumed in the forecast, although this is less likely
to materially change our assessment of restrictiveness,
given the relatively short duration of household and
business liabilities in Australia.
Key judgement #3 – Some of the
current drivers of inflation are
expected to persist and will take
time to moderate.
Some of the upside risks to the inflation outlook
identified in the August Statement have since

materialised and are now incorporated in the central
forecast for inflation. These factors have led to an
upward revision to the inflation outlook over the next
year or so and a delay in the expected timeframe for
inflation to return to the target range. Higher fuel and
other commodity prices from the Middle East conflict
are expected to put sustained upward pressure on
inflation over the coming year as these higher input
costs are passed on to consumer prices to some degree

over time. These cost pressures are expected to ease
over the latter part of the forecast period, contributing to
the decline in inflation. The change to the judgement
around the degree of domestic capacity constraints
implies more persistent inflationary pressures than in the
August forecasts, all else equal (see Chapter 2: Economic
Conditions). That is, we now assess that domestic
capacity pressures have been contributing more to
recent elevated inflation outcomes than we had

previously judged, and from this higher starting point
will contribute a bit more to inflation in the next year.
The elevated level of inflation expectations is also
projected to influence price- and wage-setting
behaviour and boost inflation and wages growth a little
more than previously expected.
We view the risks to the inflation outlook as still skewed
to the upside. Alongside the potential for global cost
pressures to be higher than assumed, it is also possible

we have misjudged the extent of capacity pressures in
the economy and the labour market, and the impact of
the recent increase in inflation expectations in price- and
wage-setting behaviour (see section 3.4 Key risks to the
outlook).

The outlook for growth in Australia’s
major trading partners (MTPs) has
been revised higher over the next
couple of years, but growth is
expected to moderate from its
current strong pace.
The MTP GDP growth forecast has been revised
upwards to 4.0 per cent in 2026, before slowing to
3.5 per cent in 2027 and 3.4 per cent in 2028 as
growth in global AI-related activity and China is
expected to moderate (Graph 3.2). Growth has been
above some estimates of potential and stronger than

expected over the past year, reflecting strong AI-related
activity and, in some economies, continued fiscal
support. These have more than offset the headwinds
from the Middle East conflict. MTP growth is expected to
moderate to around potential over 2027 and 2028.
Overall, we judge that the risks around this outlook for
MTP growth are broadly balanced. The expansion in
global AI-related investment and production may be
more persistent than Consensus forecasters have

factored in, but higher energy prices could also weigh
on activity by more than currently assumed. The outlook
for MTP growth as a whole is broadly in line with the
International Monetary Fund’s projections in the October
2026 World Economic Outlook.
The upward revision to MTP growth is concentrated
in high-income east Asian economies, which are key
exporters of AI-related goods. Strong demand for
semiconductors and other AI-enabling technologies has

supported activity in economies that are deeply
integrated into regional technology supply chains, such
as South Korea and Taiwan. While the outlook for growth
in 2027 has been revised a little higher by external
forecasters, it is still expected to moderate from the
strong pace over the past year towards estimates of
potential growth.

Growth in other advanced economies has also been
more resilient than expected, prompting upward

revisions to near-term forecasts. AI-related investment
and stronger underlying momentum have supported
activity in the United States, while defence spending and
broader resilience across services and investment have
contributed to growth in the euro area. Activity in the
United Kingdom has also held up better than expected,
supported by business investment. These tailwinds
across advanced economies have so far offset some of
the drag to activity from higher energy costs. However,

external forecasters and central banks expect
persistently elevated energy costs to reduce household
purchasing power, raise firms’ costs and increasingly
weigh on consumption and investment. In some
advanced economies, tighter financial conditions will
also weigh on growth.
3.2 The global outlook
Chapter 3  |  OutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlook
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 66

### Page 70

The outlook for GDP growth in China is broadly
unchanged from the August Statement, though
there have been some changes to the expected
timing and composition of growth. GDP growth is
forecast to be 4.6 per cent in 2026, before easing slightly
to 4.5 per cent in 2027 and 4.4 per cent in 2028. The
pick-up in growth in the September quarter is expected
to continue in the near term, given the announcement
of new policy support for infrastructure investment and

signals from authorities that supporting economic
activity will be elevated as a near-term policy priority.
Infrastructure investment is now expected to recover
from late 2026 as this support takes effect, while
structural headwinds are expected to continue weighing
on domestic demand more broadly. The timing and
effectiveness of the policy support remain uncertain,
particularly given high local government debt and a
range of barriers to some local governments

commencing infrastructure projects.
Longer-term government bond yields have risen
across advanced economies, alongside resilient
activity and persistent inflation. The increase appears
to reflect a combination of higher expected near-term
policy rates and higher longer-term real rates, reflecting
a mix of higher term premia and potentially higher
neutral rates. Market-based measures of long-term
inflation expectations remain consistent with central

bank targets. Strong investment and public demand
may be placing upward pressure on real rates, and
expectations of stronger future productivity growth –
including from the adoption of AI – may also be
contributing. Higher public and private debt issuance
and uncertainty about inflation and fiscal trajectories
may also be adding to the compensation investors
require to hold longer-duration bonds (see Box A: How
Does an Increase in Longer-term Real Yields Affect

Financial Conditions? and Box B: Developments in the
Neutral Rate of Interest).
The global inflation outlook has been revised higher,
reflecting an increase in energy prices and
continued resilience in global demand. Consensus
forecasters expect headline inflation to remain above
central bank targets into 2027 in many advanced
economies. Services disinflation has stalled, while core
goods inflation has picked up. The outlook for globally
traded goods inflation has been revised higher,

reflecting higher commodity prices and further strong
increases in export prices for semiconductors and other
AI-related goods across parts of Asia. AI-related price
pressures have not broadened materially beyond
electronics but could become more persistent or spread
to other parts of global supply chains if strong demand
continues to run ahead of the expansion in productive
capacity (see Key risk #2). The further increase in energy
prices since August could add to these upstream cost

pressures, although the extent and timing of
pass-through to consumer prices remains uncertain.
Overall, we continue to judge that the risks to global
inflation remain skewed to the upside.

Australian GDP growth is expected
to remain below our estimate of
potential growth throughout the
forecast period.
GDP growth has slowed since the end of 2025 and is
expected to remain subdued over the next couple of
years (Graph 3.3). The expected slowing in growth over
2026 reflects the effects of several headwinds weighing
on private activity, including the reduction in household
disposable incomes from higher inflation, weaker
conditions in the established housing market, and the

tightening in monetary policy, which more than offset
very strong data centre-related investment. Growth is
expected to remain below potential over the forecast
period, reflecting an assumption that financial
conditions remain restrictive (see Key judgement #2).
Consistent with the latest state and federal budgets,
public demand is expected to continue supporting
growth over the forecast period.
The risks to activity appear to be broadly balanced.

Stronger-than-expected global growth and further
strength in AI and data centre-related activity pose
upside risks to domestic growth (see Key risk #2).
However, historically low consumer and business
sentiment could be pointing to the risk that the
combined effect on domestic activity of the Middle East
conflict, the decline in housing prices and the tightening
in monetary policy could be more than assumed (see
Key risk #3).
The level of GDP at end-2028 is expected to be

broadly unchanged from the August forecasts.
Growth is projected to be slightly stronger than
expected in the near term, reflecting a much stronger
outlook for data centre-related investment compared
with the August Statement. Weighing against that is the
higher assumed market path for the cash rate, a weaker
outlook for the housing market and higher oil prices.
These pull growth in the latter part of the forecast period
a little below that expected in the August Statement.

Business investment is expected to increase strongly
and the outlook has been revised up significantly,

largely driven by data centre-related investment.
Business investment was stronger than expected in the
June quarter and firms’ surveyed expectations of future
investment were again revised higher. External
projections for the expected increase in Australia’s data
centre capacity through to 2030 have also continued to
be revised up as the size of the development pipeline
has increased. Our forecast for data centre investment is
in line with recent baseline external projections, but

below projections that assume Australia becomes a
significant regional hub for data centres. There is
considerable uncertainty around the extent of data
centre investment over coming years. This will depend
on a range of factors such as government policy,
community expectations, future demand for AI services
and grid capacity. We continue to expect that much of
this additional investment will be imported (around
70 per cent), limiting the boost to GDP growth. However,

data centre construction, as well as any potential
spillovers to higher investment in energy and utilities
infrastructure, will require domestically sourced labour
and materials. At this stage, most of this investment
pipeline is in New South Wales and Victoria where liaison
information suggests capacity constraints in the
construction sector are somewhat less binding.
3.3 Domestic outlook

At the same time, a higher assumed cash rate path, a
more significant easing in the established housing
market and higher global oil prices are expected to
weigh more on activity than in the August forecasts.
The forecasts are conditioned on a higher market path
for interest rates relative to August. In addition,
established housing prices have continued to fall more
quickly than expected, with external forecasters now
expecting a deeper downturn than assumed a few

months ago. The outlook for oil prices is also much
higher than assumed in August, which will weigh on real
household incomes. Reflecting these factors, the outlook
for household consumption and dwelling investment
has been revised lower, as has the outlook for business
investment unrelated to data centres.
The labour market is expected to
continue easing over the forecast
period, consistent with
below-potential GDP growth.
The unemployment rate is forecast to increase to

5.1 per cent by the end of 2028, which is around
1⁄4 percentage point higher than previously
expected (Graph 3.4). The unemployment rate has risen
by more than expected over recent months, and the
upward revision to forecast relative to the August
Statement largely reflects this higher starting point.
Elevated cost-of-living pressures appear to have
encouraged workers to enter or remain in the labour
force to a greater extent in recent months than was

expected at the time of the August Statement. These
pressures are expected to continue to support
participation for some time. The participation rate is
expected to decline very modestly by the end of 2028 as
easing cyclical pressures are expected to more than
offset the longer-run upward trend in female
participation. A slightly weaker outlook for demand later
in the forecast period is also contributing a little to the
upward revision to the unemployment rate profile by

late 2028.

As in previous forecasts, the

expected to remain broadly stable over the next year, in

period. The forecast employment-to-population ratio is
similar to the August Statement in the near term, before
declining more quickly to reach a lower level by the end
of the forecast period.

Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 69

### Page 73

The economy is expected to move
into balance in 2027, with spare
capacity then emerging in the
economy over the rest of the forecast
period.
Below-potential output growth and an easing in
labour market conditions are expected to see the
economy move from a position of excess demand to
around balance in 2027, before some spare capacity
emerges. The timing of the return to balance is slightly
later than in the August Statement, as a result of the
upgrade to near-term GDP growth.

However, there is considerable uncertainty around
the assessment of when the economy will return to
balance and the extent of spare capacity that
emerges. The impact of conflict-related costs on
inflation over the period ahead will also make it more
difficult to assess the degree of underlying capacity
pressures in the domestic economy. Our assumption for
potential output growth over the next couple of years
assumes productivity growth will pick up gradually (see

Key risk #4). However, with the level of labour
productivity remaining around the same rate as prior to
the pandemic – implying near-zero productivity growth
on average over that period – continued weak
productivity outcomes may be constraining the ability of
firms to grow their supply capacity without generating
inflationary pressure.
Wages growth is expected to pick up
a little in the near term, then
moderate gradually from 2027 as
labour market conditions ease.

The forecast for year-ended wages growth has been
revised a little higher across the forecast period. In
the near term, nominal wages growth is expected to be
supported by the effect of higher-than-expected
capacity pressures in the first half of the year as well as
the Fair Work Commission’s award wage decision (which
had already been incorporated in the August forecasts).
The influence of both higher-than-expected inflation
outcomes and inflation expectations on wage

negotiations is expected to add further upward pressure
on wages growth relative to the August forecast. Some
large wage increases in enterprise bargaining
agreements are expected to support wages growth in
the near term, although the timing of pay increases for
these agreements may contribute to volatility in
quarterly outcomes, particularly for the public sector.
Wages growth expectations from firms in the RBA’s
liaison program are elevated and suggest little

easing over the year ahead (see Box C: Insights from
Liaison). This is consistent with the central forecast for
wages growth. Growth is expected to moderate from
mid-2027 onwards, reflecting easing labour market
conditions and lower inflation outcomes. Wages growth
is expected to have eased to 2.9 per cent in year-ended
terms by end-2028 (Graph 3.5).

Unit labour cost growth is forecast to
be elevated in the near term, before
easing over the second half of the
forecast period.
Year-ended growth in unit labour costs (ULCs) is
expected to increase in coming quarters, reflecting
weak productivity growth and higher wages growth.
Year-ended growth in ULCs is then expected to
moderate from late 2027 onwards, alongside slowing
nominal wages growth and the pick-up in productivity
growth to its assumed medium-term trend growth rate

of 0.7 per cent per year (Graph 3.6). Nominal ULCs are
the measure of labour costs most relevant for firms’ cost
of production and so for inflation outcomes. Year-ended
ULCs growth is expected to remain above 2.5 per cent –
the rate that is consistent with inflation being
sustainably at target – until late in the forecast period.

The forecast for underlying inflation
has been revised higher over the
next year, mainly owing to higher
conflict-related cost pressures and
higher-than-expected domestic

Trimmed mean inflation is expected to remain
above 3 per cent until the end of 2027, easing
gradually to reach 21⁄2 per cent around mid-2028
(Graph 3.7). Year-ended underlying inflation is expected

to remain elevated at a little above 31⁄2 per cent until
mid-2027, reflecting existing capacity pressures in the
economy and the pass-through of higher
conflict-related costs to consumer prices. Elevated
inflation expectations are also contributing to
underlying inflation over the coming year or so. From
mid-2027 onwards, inflation is expected to ease due to
the emergence of spare capacity in the economy and
labour market and as conflict-related cost effects begin

to unwind.

### Page 75

Cost pressures related to the Middle East conflict are
expected to place more upward pressure on
underlying inflation over the year ahead than
previously assumed. A possible re-escalation in the
conflict had been flagged as an upside risk to inflation in
the August Statement. Since then, oil and fuel prices
have increased by much more than had been assumed
and are now assumed to remain at a higher level over
the forecast period. We expect higher global energy

prices will contribute to higher domestic inflationary
pressures in late 2026 and early 2027, even as domestic
activity slows and the labour market eases (Graph 3.8).
The judgement around the degree of pass-through from
global energy prices to domestic prices is broadly similar
to what had been assumed in August. It is consistent
with information from liaison and the Business Outlook
Scenario Survey (BOSS) in September, where firms
reported plans to continue raising prices in response to

past cost increases arising partially from the conflict.
From mid-2027 onwards, we expect cost effects to
unwind in net terms, albeit to a lesser extent than
assumed in the August Statement as oil prices are now
assumed to remain at a higher level than previously
expected and refined margins are assumed to remain
elevated for longer (see Key judgement #1). The balance
of risks to conflict-related inflationary pressures remains
skewed to the upside, particularly as crude oil and

refined fuel prices could remain elevated or rise further
(see Key risk #1).
Relative to the August forecasts, domestic capacity
pressures are also expected to exert a bit more
upward pressure on inflation over the year ahead.
We now judge that stronger-than-expected domestic
capacity pressures explain more of the recent
unexpected strength in underlying inflation (see
Chapter 2: Economic Conditions). Those higher capacity
pressures are also expected to contribute a little more to

inflation over the year ahead. This upward pressure is
expected to ease gradually over the forecast period, as
spare capacity emerges in the economy and the
labour market.

Elevated inflation expectations are expected to

contribute to inflationary pressure over the forecast
period, supporting the pass-through of firms’ costs
to consumer prices. Several measures of short- and
medium-term inflation expectations have increased
alongside the recent increases in fuel prices, and some
measures of longer-term expectations have also drifted
higher (see Chapter 2: Economic Conditions). We judge
that elevated expectations will contribute to higher
realised inflation by supporting expected cost growth,

making firms more willing to pass these higher costs
through to prices, and placing upward pressure on
wages growth. If the conflict in the Middle East is more
protracted than assumed in our baseline forecasts, there
is a risk that inflation expectations may increase further
(see Key risk #1).

Headline inflation has been revised
higher in the near term due to
stronger growth in fuel prices and
higher underlying inflation.
Headline inflation is expected to remain above 3 per
cent until late 2027, which is around three quarters
longer than in the August forecast, and reach the
midpoint of the 2–3 per cent inflation target range a
bit later than expected in August (Graph 3.9). Headline
inflation is expected to peak at 4.5 per cent in the
December quarter this year due to

higher-than-expected fuel prices. The assumed decline
in global oil prices is expected to put downward
pressure on headline inflation from early 2027. Over the
remainder of the forecast period, headline inflation has
been revised higher relative to our August forecasts,
reflecting higher underlying inflation (Graph 3.10).
Headline inflation is expected to reach the midpoint of
the target in mid-2028 as capacity pressures and
conflict-related costs ease.

### Page 77

The staff assess that the risks to the domestic inflation
outlook are skewed to the upside, despite becoming
more balanced since the August Statement as some of
the risks identified then have been incorporated into the
central forecast. In August, we identified three main
sources of upside risk to the global and domestic
inflation outlook: a re-escalation of the Middle East
conflict; domestic capacity pressures being greater than
assumed; and the global AI boom generating more of an

inflationary impetus than assumed. Since then, the risk
of a re-escalation of the Middle East conflict has
crystallised and staff have re-evaluated the extent of
domestic capacity constraints; both of which have led to
upward revisions to the central inflation forecast over the
next year. However, there is still uncertainty around the
outlook for global energy prices, the role of inflation
expectations in price and wage dynamics as well as the
impact on inflation from AI activity.

The staff also identified in August that there were upside
risks to global and domestic economic activity from
AI-related demand, but that domestic economic activity
could be weaker if the Middle East conflict and the
decline in housing prices affected the economy by more
than assumed in the central forecast. These remain key
risks to the outlook.
Key risk #1 – High inflation could be
more persistent if energy prices
remain elevated, or if inflation

expectations increase or start to
have a greater bearing on wage- and
price-setting.
We have modelled a scenario where crude oil
remains around US$100 per barrel and refined
product prices remain at elevated levels over the
forecast period. Many external forecasters are revising
higher their outlook for crude oil and refined product
prices, on concerns that some of the key margins of
adjustment in global energy markets that have helped
to cushion the impact of the disruption to oil supply and

global activity are reaching their limits (see Chapter 2:
Economic Conditions and Key judgement #1).
Persistently higher global oil prices would leave
domestic inflation higher for a longer period than in
the central forecast. Higher oil prices would directly
increase fuel prices and firms’ input costs, with the latter
gradually passed through to consumer prices as firms
adjust to persistently higher costs. By contrast, the
central forecast assumes a decline in oil prices, which

starts to ease cost pressures related to the Middle East
conflict from mid-2027, contributing to lower inflation
over time (Graph 3.11). Higher inflation also raises
inflation expectations in the scenario, reinforcing
inflationary pressures over the forecast period. Assuming
that the cash rate and exchange rate follow the same
path that underpins the central forecasts, underlying
inflation would be around 23⁄4 per cent by
end-2028 rather than being around the 21⁄2 per cent.

3.4 Key risks to the outlook
Chapter 3  |  OutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlook
Reserve Bank of A ustralia  |  Statement on Monetar y Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026Statement on Monetary Policy  |  November 2026 74

### Page 78

It is possible that inflation expectations could rise
even further or that inflation expectations provide
greater impetus to wages growth and inflation
through second-round effects. The central forecasts
and scenario assume that the increase in households’
and firms’ inflation expectations do exert some upward
pressure on inflation and wages growth. However, given
households and firms are paying particularly close
attention to inflation, it is possible that inflation

expectations will influence price- and wage-setting
decisions by more than assumed. This heightened
attentiveness to inflation could also make expectations
more persistent and more responsive to realised
inflation. With headline inflation forecast to rise further in
the near term, this increases the risk that inflation
expectations remain higher for longer than assumed in
our central forecast.
Key risk #2 – The global AI demand
boom may continue to drive up

global growth and inflation.
MTP growth may not moderate as expected over the
next few years if AI-demand growth continues at the
same pace. It is possible that growth forecasts could
continue to be upwardly revised by Consensus
forecasters if the current upswing in AI-related
investment (and spillovers associated with the boom) is
more persistent than currently assumed. The rise in
interest rates across the yield curve in many advanced
economies may reflect higher neutral rates – that is,

expectations that demand will prove more resilient and
inflation more persistent than currently assumed. Higher
longer-term real rates may partly reflect expectations of
stronger future productivity growth, including from the
adoption of AI. Further investment in anticipation of
higher productivity could also support demand and add
to capacity pressures over the next few years, while most
of the productivity gains are likely to manifest beyond
the next two years (at least in Australia).

The ramp-up of investment in data centres may also
contribute to capacity and pricing pressures
domestically, particularly by drawing on resources in
the construction industry. These price pressures could
be stronger than currently expected as the forecast
pipeline of investment is worked through, or if
investment proves stronger than expected.
Key risk #3 – Domestic economic
activity could be weaker than
expected.
It is possible that the headwinds affecting

households and businesses over the next year could
have a larger adverse effect than assumed. The
central forecasts assume that higher oil prices, higher
interest rates and lower housing prices all weigh on
domestic economic activity broadly in line with average
historical relationships. Timely indicators of spending
remain consistent with this assumption, with no
evidence of a material slowing in consumption growth
in recent months. However, the very weak levels of

consumer and business sentiment could be pointing to
a more pronounced slowing in household and business
spending growth than assumed.
We have calibrated a downside domestic activity
scenario where there is a larger response in
household and business spending to these
headwinds. There is a range of estimates for the effects
of these headwinds in the literature, highlighting the
uncertainty around their impact on economic activity.
Drawing on this evidence, the scenario uses larger

responses than assumed in the central forecast, thereby
identifying a plausible downside outlook for domestic
activity. In the scenario, firms feel forced to pass on the
conflict-related costs to prices as in the central forecast;
but households respond more cautiously to the
resultant pressures on real income and wealth, reducing
discretionary consumption by more than assumed in the
central forecast. Such an effect on spending might be
compounded by a tightening in borrowing constraints

and incentives for housing investors. The scenario
assumes firms respond to weaker demand and margin
pressure with a sharper pullback on (non-data centre)
investment and hiring. Overall, the level of GDP is around
1.0 per cent lower than the central forecasts
by end-2028.
The larger pullback in household and business
spending results in weaker activity and employment
growth, but only modestly lower inflation than in
the central forecast, assuming interest rates and the

exchange rate follow the same path as the central
forecast. The greater caution exhibited by households
pushes the gross household saving ratio up gradually to
a peak of around 17 per cent, where it remains over the
remainder of the forecast period. The greater reduction
Chapter 3  |  OutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlookOutlook

in hiring pushes the unemployment rate 0.3 percentage
points higher by end-2028, creating more spare capacity
in the domestic economy than in the central forecast
(Graph 3.12). The additional spare capacity in the
economy and the labour market reduces inflationary
pressures somewhat, but the assumption of cost
pass-through by firms limits this effect. Overall, trimmed
mean inflation reaches 21⁄2 per cent only one quarter
earlier than in the central forecast.

Key risk #4 – There remains
considerable uncertainty about the
degree of capacity pressures in the
domestic economy and labour
market.
Despite the recent reassessment, it is possible that

we are still understating the extent of capacity
pressures. While we assess that capacity constraints in
the economy and labour market have been tighter than
previously judged, model-based estimates of the
unemployment rate that is consistent with low and
stable inflation continue to point to greater tightness in
labour market conditions than our central assessment. In
addition, if productivity growth were to remain subdued
over the forecast period, rather than pick up modestly as

assumed, it may suggest that the economy’s supply
capacity would be lower than currently expected. The
implications for inflation would depend on how
households and businesses adjust their spending
and expectations.
Conversely, capacity pressures may be weaker than
expected. The broader suite of full employment
indicators points to somewhat less labour market
tightness than our central assessment. It is also possible
that more of the recent strength in wages growth and

inflation reflects factors other than excess demand, such
as conflict-related cost effects or higher inflation
expectations. In these cases, wages growth and inflation
may moderate more quickly than in the central forecast.
Productivity growth could also recover more quickly
than assumed, including if the anticipated benefits of AI
adoption and other technological developments are
realised sooner.
There is also a risk that the unemployment rate
increases by more than expected if labour supply

remains stronger than assumed. There is a risk that
elevated cost-of-living pressures could support labour
force participation for longer, or to a greater extent, than
assumed. If so, stronger labour supply could result in a
higher unemployment rate than forecast, even if labour
demand evolves broadly as expected.

Document 350: France_ double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans ratio (2026-10-08)

Source PDF: all report pdf/France_ double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and stable non-performing loans ratio (2026-10-08).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-08
Pages: 1
Status: ok

### Page 1

Paris, 8 October 2026

EcoFlash

France: double-digit growth in business creations, a slowdown in the rise in bankruptcies and 
stable non-performing loans ratio

Business creations rose by 12% y/y over the first eight months of 2026 1. This is significantly higher than the 5.3% y/y 
growth recorded in 2025. This increase was driven by sectors linked to information and communication technologies.

Meanwhile, business bankruptcies rose by 5.3% y/y between January and July, according to data from the Banque de 
France (compared with the 3.6% y/y increase for the whole of 2025). The rise was more pronounced in the 1st quarter (+8% y/y) 
before slowing down. This slowdown could persist, as, according to Altares, payment delays have fallen to an average of 12.3 
days in the second quarter of 2026, compared with 14.1 days a year earlier (and 13.1 days for the European average).

Rising bankruptcies are more heavily affecting the services sector, which is also experiencing slightly stronger growth 
in business creations. This correlation is particularly noticeable in the retail sector, as business creations have risen by 13% 
since the start of 2026, while bankruptcies are up by 3%. In construction and manufacturing, business bankruptcies have been 
falling since the second quarter of 2026.

The average ratio of non -performing corporate loans has remained stable at a moderately high level for the past year  
(4.0% of total loans and advances in Q2 2026). In construction, where activity is more cyclical than in other sectors, the ra tio of 
non-performing loans has reached its highest level since 2019, standing at 9.5%.

The proportion of loans that have deteriorated but are not yet non-performing (“Stage 2” according to IFRS 9 terminology) 
is stable, standing at 15.4% q/q, and remains below its all -time high (16.1%). This indicator, which helps to anticipate trends in 
non-performing loans, is relatively encouraging.

Despite a significant increase since 2021, the economic weight of bankruptcies remains, on average, below its peak in 
Q1 2025 (0.53 compared with 0.61, respectively). This indicator generally allows bankruptcies to be weighted based on the size 
of the businesses. However, the economic weight of bankrupted small businesses is at a historically high level (2.11).

Take a look at our EcoCharts on trends in business creations and bankruptcies in France and the 
associated risks. It will be updated on a quarterly basis.

Thomas Humblot 
Economiste, team: Banking economics 
thomas.humblot@bnpparibas.com

Stéphane Colliac 
Head of Advanced economies, France 
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

Nothing To Fear But Fear Itself And FOMO, FEMO, FOGO & FOJO

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Nothing To Fear But Fear Itself
And FOMO, FEMO, FOGO &
FOJO
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 3 Nov 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Игорь Кеся / Unsplash
I. Three Shades Of Fear
Monday's issue of The Wall Street Journal featured a story titled, "Hedge
Fund Elliott Warns of FOGO: Fear of Getting Obliterated in the Market."
That has inspired us today to coin "FOJO," or fear of jumping out of the
market. FOMO, of course, is the fear of missing out on a bull market. Only
a few months ago, we felt the need to coin "FEMO," or fabulous earnings
momentum to describe this year's earnings-led bull market.

FOMO is reflected in the forward price-to-earnings (P/E) multiple, while
FEMO is captured by forward earnings per share (E). So far this year,
FEMO has been booming, but FOMO has been fading. S&P 500 forward
earnings surged 31.3% ytd through November 2, lifting the index 10.2%
despite a 13.8% contraction in its forward P/E (chart). Earnings
momentum has more than offset multiple contraction.
Ironically, one reason FOMO has declined may be FEMO itself. Investors

fear that analysts' earnings expectations might be irrationally exuberant.
We share that concern. Analysts' consensus S&P 500 operating earnings
are currently projected to reach $494 by 2028, well exceeding our $450
forecast and approaching our $500 target for 2030 (chart).

### Page 3

Sentiment about the AI trade has shifted from euphoria to skepticism as
financial, political, social, and physical constraints on the AI buildout
intensify. Investors fear these could slow AI investment and force analysts
to lower their exuberant earnings expectations.
The market seems to recognize that risk. Despite analysts' soaring long-
term earnings growth (LTEG) expectations this year, the S&P 500's
forward P/E has declined to 18.1 (chart). Investors are increasingly

reluctant to pay up for earnings growth that may prove unsustainable.
Doubts about future FEMO are weighing on today's FOMO. (During the
1999 Tech Bubble, the forward P/E rose faster than LTEG.)
The good news is that FOJO might now be driving the bull market. While
investors are increasingly concerned about the AI trade, they are reluctant

### Page 4

to bail out of the stock market. Instead, they are rotating aggressively out
of technology and into other sectors. We remain bullish, targeting 7,900
for the S&P 500 by year-end, based on our 2027 EPS forecast of $425
and a forward P/E of 18.6.
II. The Roaring Economy
Today's economic data clearly illustrate why we remain fundamentally
bullish. Despite stock market jitters, the US economy continues to roar.
Consider the following:
(1) JOLTS. Job openings rose to 7.5 million in September, the highest

since May (chart). Total hires rose to 5.4 million, the highest since March.
Quits surged to 3.4 million, the highest since March 2025. Layoffs fell to
2.0 million, the lowest since March 2025. The message is clear: More job
openings, stronger hiring, rising quits, and fewer layoffs all point to
strengthening labor market conditions.
Job openings in cyclically sensitive industries are upbeat. Manufacturing
openings rose to 584,000 in September, the highest since December

2023, while construction openings have also been trending higher (chart).
Both industries are benefiting from the AI infrastructure investment boom.
Meanwhile, retail trade openings are trending higher, reflecting strong
consumer demand.

### Page 5

(2) Consumer spending. With the labor market remaining healthy, it is
hardly surprising that consumers continue to spend. Redbook same-store
retail sales rose 9.8% y/y during the week of October 30 (chart).
(3) Durable goods orders. Durable goods orders rose 1.9% m/m in
September. Orders excluding transportation increased 0.5%, while core
capital goods orders (nondefense ex-aircraft), a key gauge of business
investment, rose 0.6% and 13.7% y/y (charts).

### Page 6

The major components of durable goods orders are at record highs
(chart).

### Page 7

Orders for machinery necessary to operate data centers are especially
strong (chart).
Meanwhile, unfilled machinery orders climbed to $155.1 billion in
September, approaching their all-time high and signaling a robust pipeline
of future production (chart).
Unfilled orders for computers and electronic products rose to a record
$162.4 billion in September (chart). The increase highlights robust
demand for AI-related infrastructure.

### Page 8

clause.

Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026EDITORIAL
WHY CHINA RESISTS REBALANCING ITS GROWTH MODEL?
China has never exported as much as it does now, and its trade surplus has never been higher. At the same time, its
economic growth is slowing, held back by weak domestic demand. The imbalances in China’s growth model, and the ex-
ternal imbalances they give rise to, are therefore continuing to worsen. With Europe taking a tougher line and the United

States, with which China shares nothing more than ‘strategic stability’, the standoff looks set to continue. However, the
European Union and China would have a great deal to lose from a lasting deterioration in their relationship. It is in the
interests of both parties to seek to restore the balance. To this end, it is important to understand Beijing’s objectives and
the constraints facing China. Why does the country resist adjusting its growth model? Whilst exports are a vital asset for

sustaining economic activity and asserting China’s technological and industrial power, this strategy also comes at a cost.
IMBALANCES ARE WORSENING
A K-shaped economy. China’s economic growth slowed to 4.6% year-
on-year (y/y) over the first three quarters of 2026, from 5.0% in 2025.
More worryingly, the gap is widening between weak domestic demand
and the very strong performance of exports, driven by artificial intelli-
gence equipment and green technologies. Furthermore, the dynamism

of export-oriented industries has not, so far, had any positive knock-on
effects on other sectors or on households.
Domestic demand is struggling. The property crisis, now in its sixth
year, and a sluggish labour market are weighing on household confi -
dence and consumption. Since the start of 2026, households have been
prioritising debt reduction (bank loans to households have fallen by
1% y/y), house sales have continued to fall, and retail sales have been

stagnating (Chart 1). On the investment front, the decline is becoming
more pronounced and widespread: it is affecting not only the property
sector but also infrastructure and many manufacturing sectors (with
the exception of the textile industry, transport and communications,
and electronic and electrical equipment).
Exports remain very buoyant. Exports continue to be the main driver of
growth (their net contribution is expected to account for around a third

of real GDP growth in 2026, as it did in 2024–2025). Against a backdrop
of rebound in global trade, Chinese companies maintained their market
share at 14.6% of total exports (measured in US dollars) in the first
half of 2026, as they had in 2025. Growth in goods exports accelerated
(21.9% y/y in USD over the first nine months, compared with 5.2% in
2025), driven by both volumes and prices. These have rebounded after
three years of decline, driven by rising costs of raw materials and the

prices of goods required for AI, but they have increased at a slower rate
than the global price average. Exports from high-tech sectors (batte -
ries, electric vehicles, robots and AI equipment) are posting the highest
growth rates (+55.8% y/y for all AI-related goods over the first nine
months of 2026).
Record external surpluses. Imports, which had been stagnating since
2022, have been recovering since the start of the year (up 27.2% y/y
over the first nine months), also driven by both volumes and prices.

The countries benefiting from this rebound in the Chinese market are
commodity exporters and Asian producers integrated into AI value
chains (South Korea in particular). Otherwise, China has significantly
reduced its reliance on imports over the past few years. Overall, the
trade surplus has grown at a slower rate in 2026 than in previous years.
Nevertheless, it has still reached a record high: USD1,249 billion over
the twelve months to September 2026, which is three times its 2019

level (see Chart 2).

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Meanwhile, the current account surplus is expected to approach a re -
cord level of USD 800 billion in 2026, representing nearly 4% of China’s
GDP (a ratio already seen in the late 2000s). As a proportion of global
GDP , this is unprecedented (at 0.6%).
Imbalances set to persist. The imbalances inherent in the growth mo-
del are likely to persist, fuelled notably by the strong price and non-

price competitiveness of Chinese goods. This competitiveness remains
underpinned by: i/ innovation efforts and the move up the value chain
in high-tech sectors and the low-carbon transition; ii/ low production
costs (labour, low-cost credit, land and energy, tax benefits 1); iii/ the
yuan’s exchange rate. Its slow appreciation since mid-2025 has not
offset the cumulative depreciation from 2022 to 2025 (nominal and
real depreciation due to low inflation), and its real effective exchange

rate (REER) remains 10% below its 2021 average. At the end of 2025,
the IMF estimated that the REER was undervalued by between 12%
and 20%.
Beijing’s economic strategy is exacerbating imbalances. The 15th Five-
Year Plan2, launched this year, prioritises innovation, high-tech (ex -
port-oriented) sectors and technological self-reliance above all else,
whilst reforms to the social security system and private consumption
remain on the back burner (see Chart 3). Furthermore, fiscal policy,

which had provided moderate support for household spending for a
time in 2025, was tightened in 2026 (until the recent announcements
of new measures for the final quarter).
WHY BEIJING IS DIGGING ITS HEELS IN
Beijing is not giving ground on any key issues. Faced with trading
partners calling on China to rebalance its economic model, reduce in -
dustrial subsidies and strengthen the yuan, Beijing responds that the
trade surplus reflects the competitiveness of its goods, points to the

United States’ insufficient savings, and uses critical materials as a bar-
gaining chip in negotiations.
The need for growth. Exports are a key driver of growth at a time when
the Chinese economy is undergoing a period of transition. The country
has moved away from its pre-2022 model, which was based on in -
vestment in property and traditional industries and financed by debt.
Growth is now driven by ‘new productive forces’, and exports must pro-

vide producers with the market that domestic demand does not. Fur -
thermore, innovation and the roll-out of AI must stimulate productivity
gains and offset the impact of the shrinking labour force on economic
growth. It is essential to limit the slowdown of growth in order to sup-
port job creation and absorb the economy’s excess debt.
The strategic argument. Exports are a means of asserting China’s in -
dustrial and technological supremacy, weakening its competitors, and

strengthening ‘national security’. They place China at the heart of va -
lue chains in the most strategic industries, such as AI and the low-car-
bon transition. Furthermore, the pursuit of self-sufficiency and import
substitution — which exacerbate external imbalances — also help to
build national sovereignty by reducing dependencies.
A political and fiscal choice. Expanding the social safety net is seen as
a solution for providing long-term support for household consumption3

by reducing their savings rate (estimated at 35.7% of disposable income
in 2025, which is more than double the average rate for the euro area).
1 According to the IMF, the cost of China’s industrial policy amounts to about 4% of GDP . See IMF, ‘Industrial Policy in China: Quantification and Impact on Misalloca-
tion’, August 2025: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229017855.001
2 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, China’s 15th Five-Year Plan: prioritising power over addressing imbalances

3 The IMF estimates, for example, that doubling social expenditure (health, education, social services and basic pensions) in rural areas could reduce savings and
raise the ratio of private consumption to GDP by 1.8 pp in the medium term, at an annual fiscal cost of 1% of GDP . See IMF, People’s Republic of China 2025 Article IV,
February 2026: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229038911.002
However, this is not a priority for Beijing, certainly due to ‘ideologi -

cal’ mistrust of the welfare state and a desire for fiscal prudence.
A major reform of the healthcare or pension systems would place a
strain on public finances in both the short and long term, particularly
as the population ages. Whilst the central government remains in a
sound financial position, local governments are already under strain
following several years of falling land sales proceeds, high deficits and
rising debt. The authorities do not have a large fiscal room for ma -

noeuvre and wish to preserve it in anticipation of future shocks.
LIMITATIONS AND POSSIBLE ADJUSTMENTS
In China, a society gripped by anxiety… Economic imbalances are ta -
king their toll on households. The emphasis placed on manufacturing
rather than services is having a negative impact on employment and
wages (the share of value added allocated to employees’ pay is esti -
mated at 50% in the services sector and 41% in manufacturing). The
downturn in the property market is eroding households’ wealth, and

their savings are mainly held in low-yield bank accounts. Finally, social
spending is rising slowly (by 5.2% a year since 2020, compared with
9.7% over the previous five years).
… and enterprises under pressure. The low prices of Chinese goods,
both for export and on the domestic market — resulting from excess
production capacity and ‘involution’ — are eroding corporate profit
margins. Total profits for industrial enterprises have recovered this
year but they are highly concentrated, particularly in AI-related sec -

tors, while the proportion of industrial companies reporting losses has
continued to rise (30%, compared with 26% in 2022). These vulnerabili-
ties are having increasing contagion effects on the rest of the economy,
notably through worsening payment delays and rising pressure on the
profitability of financial institutions.

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Abroad, trading partners are becoming increasingly intolerant. This
is particularly the case of the United States and the European Union 4,
which have absorbed 24% and 28% respectively of China’s trade surplus
over the last twelve months (data from China’s General Administration
of Customs). Tensions between the US and China could rise again in
2027 when the current truce (which has mainly led to a reduction in

tariffs following the peak in H1 2025) comes to an end. Against a back-
drop of competition for technological supremacy and industrial sove -
reignty, the trend towards decoupling between China and the US could
continue, thereby reducing market opportunities for Chinese producers.
Europe, for its part, is reinforcing its strategic approach and expanding
its toolkit of measures, moving towards more targeted restrictions and
stricter market access conditions, especially in case of serious market

distortions). Rather than pursuing a strategy of decoupling, the EU is
seeking to reduce risks and provide greater protection for critical sec -
tors (including the automotive and chemical industries) without clo -
sing its market and whilst continuing to attract Chinese investment.
This strategy will also lead to tensions. With imbalances becoming
increasingly unsustainable, what course of action would make it pos -
sible to avoid a sudden adjustment precipitated by episodes of crises?

4 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, When it comes to China, Europe should focus less on its trade deficits and more on its investments
A ‘concerted’ adjustment is difficult to envisage. A negotiated adjust -
ment, along the lines of the 1985 Plaza agreements, seems unlikely in
the current international and military context. Furthermore, China and
its trading partners largely disagree on the diagnosis and the causes of
the imbalances. Moreover, a simple revaluation of the yuan would, on

its own, have only a limited effect on China’s competitiveness.
A gradual adjustment may be possible… in the long term. However, the
reduction of imbalances will need to come both from partner countries
(through increased savings) and from China. In the short term, China
can ease trade tensions by investing more abroad, which is all the
easier given that it has huge current account surpluses. This would
enable it to maintain its access to foreign markets and support the

production capacity of its trading partners. More structural changes
(a more flexible exchange rate regime, less fiscal support for indus -
tries and more support for households) will only be feasible once Bei -
jing considers that the costs of its export-led model will outweigh the
strategic gains. A key challenge for trading partners will be to work out
how to shift this cost-benefit balance.
Christine Peltier
christine.peltier@bnpparibas.com

十月 29, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新  IC設計 ‧ 台灣

瑞昱 
(2379.TW /2379 TT)

規格升級與價格調整抵銷 PC/NB 需求疲軟 
重要訊息 
瑞昱召開 3Q26 法說會。  
評論及分析  
3Q26 財報符合預期。 瑞昱 3Q26 營收達 377 億元，季增 0.5%、年增
27.9%，主因客戶需求保持 穩健，其中又 以網通產品為主要成長動能。儘管
PC 需求疲弱，營收仍維持高檔，係受惠： (1)高規格產品占比提升帶動平均
單價提升；及(2)價格調整部分轉嫁成本上升。上述因素亦支撐 3Q26 毛利率
達 47.4%，季增 0.4 個百分點，年減 2.2 個百分點。在業外收益 6.06 億元
挹注下，使 3Q26 EPS 達 8.05 元，符合我們預估。  
4Q26 PC 需求續弱，但產品組合與價格調整可望抵銷季節性衰退。 管理層表
示 4Q26 動能保持穩健，在產品組合改善與價格調整推升 ASP 下，營收不致
於出現往常的季節性下滑。不過 PC 需求預期將進一步走弱，主因： (1)客戶
在 1H26 提前拉貨；(2) DRAM 與 CPU 成本上漲壓抑終端需求。 通路庫存大
致健康，未見刻意囤貨跡象。如同我們在 十月 Radar monthly 中所述，具備
規格升級、產品組合改善與訂價能力的台灣 IC 設計公司，營收及毛利率相
對較能獲得支撐。毛利率方面，管理層重申 2H26 毛利率將逐步回升，我們
亦認為漲價效益逐步反映亦 可提供支撐。我們上修 4Q26 營收預估至 378 億
元，季增 0.2%、年增 43.8%；毛利率預估上修至 47.9%，季增 0.5 個百分
點、年減 0.2 個百分點。  
2027 年規格升級支撐 營收成長，成本壓力則限縮毛利率改善空間。 管理層
預期 2027 年營收將穩健成長，係受惠 ：(1)產品組合優化與價格調整推升整
體 ASP；(2)規格升級，包括 Wi-Fi 7 滲透率由 2026 年約 30%提升至約
40%、multi -gig 乙太網路與可管理型交換器採用率擴大，以及 Wi-Fi 8 於
2H27 開始量產爬坡； (3)車用乙太網路與 100G 光通訊 PHY 等新產品，惟
PC 仍為主要不確定性因素，目前尚未看見全面復甦的跡象。成本方面，晶
圓代工與封測成本仍具 不確定性、 AI 伺服器持續排擠產能，供給預期要到
2027 年才會明顯改善。管理層 表示成本上升幅度已超出公司可合理吸收的
範圍，因此自 6 月起即逐案與客戶討論價格調整，但目標為維 持毛利率穩
定，不會單純為拉高毛利率而漲價。綜合考量：(1)晶圓代工與封測成本壓力
將持續； (2)價格調整僅能部分轉嫁； (3)產品組合與規格升級的效益，我們
上修 2027 年營收預估 至 1,602 億元，年增 7.2%，下修毛利 率預估至
48.4%，年增 0.4 個百分點。 
投資建議  
基於： (1)營收預估上修，反映漲價與規格升級； (2)毛利率預估下修 ，反映
晶圓代工及封測成本壓力僅部分轉嫁 ，整體而言我們 分別上修 2026-27 年
EPS 預估至 32.50 元(年增 13.0%)，及 37.86 元(年增 16.5%)。目標價小幅
上調至 680 元，基於 2027 年 EPS 預估 18 倍不變。維持「持有」評等。  
投資風險  
終端需求不振；供應鏈成本上揚對 ASP、利潤率形成壓力；競爭加劇。

台灣 瑞昱

十月 29, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  5

ESG 圖表定義  
項目 定義 說明 
能源使用 
直接與間接能源消耗總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司運營範圍內消耗之能源總量 
- 能源使用總量 = 直接能源消耗總量 + 間接能源消耗量 
- 總能源使用量包括購買的能源、生產的能源 
- 就公用事業而言，輸電/電網損耗為其業務活動之一部分，被視為總能源消耗，數據不包括
為滿足能源使用而生產的電力(公用事業為出售而生產) 
- 就公用事業而言，用於能源生產的煤、天然氣或核能等原料不屬於「總能源使用」項下

購買之再生能源 
Primary再生能源購買總量(十億焦耳) 
- 公司各種來源之能源消耗量與購買的能源中屬於自然界可再生者(太陽能、風能、水能、生
物質能、地熱能)之量 
- 如無證據顯示再生能源由公司生產，則所報告的能源數據視為購買的再生能源

再生能源使用率 再生能源占總能源消耗量之比例  
CO2 約當排放量 
直接 CO2與 CO2約當排放量(公噸) 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量(範圍 1排放量) 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳(CO2)、甲烷(CH4)、一氧化二氮(N2O)、氫氟碳化物(HFCS)、全氟化合
物(PFCS)、六氟化硫(SF6)、三氟化氮(NF3)

CO2 約當排放量營收比 
直接 CO2與 CO2約當排放量(公噸)銷售(百萬元)比 
- 公司擁有或控制的來源的直接排放量(範圍 1排放量) 
- 相關氣體：二氧化碳(CO2)、甲烷(CH4)、一氧化二氮(N2O)、氫氟碳化物(HFCS)、全氟化合
物(PFCS)、六氟化硫(SF6)、三氟化氮(NF3)

廢棄物總量 
廢棄物總量(公噸) 
- 總廢棄物 = 無害廢棄物 + 有害廢棄物 
- 僅考慮固體廢棄物，但如液體廢棄物以公噸為單位呈報，則會將其加入求得包含液體廢棄
物之總量 
- 對於採礦、石油與天然氣等行業，tailings、廢石、煤、飛灰等廢棄物亦考慮在內

廢棄物回收率 
公司呈報的廢棄物回收率 
- 廢棄物回收率 = 廢棄物回收量/總廢棄物*100 
- 廢棄物轉化為能源或經由廢棄物焚燒產生能源視為廢棄物回收 
- 經由堆肥回收的廢棄物視為回收之廢棄物

總取水量 
總取水量(立方公尺) 
- 由呈報組織直接或經水公司等中介機構從任何水源抽取的總水量 
- 水井、城鎮/公用事業/市政用水、河水、地表水等不同之水源均予以考慮

環保支出 
環保支出總金額 
- 所有用於環境保護，或防止、減少、控制環境因素、影響、危害的投資與支出，亦包括處
置、處理、衛生、清理支出

員工流動率 
員工流動率 
- 包括任何原因(自願或非自願)離開公司的員工，如辭職、退休、自然離職/死亡、醫療失
能、冗員、裁員、重組、解僱、裁減或定期合約到期 
- 員工流動率 = (離開之員工/平均員工人數)*100 
- 平均員工人數 = (本年末員工人數 + 去年末員工人數)/2 
- 本財年末員工數 = 上個財年末員工數 + 新員工數 - 離開之員工數

女性經理 
女性經理百分比 
- 女性經理占公司經理的百分比 
- 如有不同階層之百分比，如最高階、高階、中階、初階，則會考慮中階女性經理之百分比 
- 女性經理百分比 = 女性經理人數/經理總數*100

女性員工 
女性員工百分比 
- 女性員工占公司員工總數的百分比 
- 女性員工百分比 = 女性人數/員工總數*100

教育訓練總時數 
所有員工教育訓練總時數 
- 僅考慮員工教育訓練時數 
- 包括一般員工所有類型的教育訓練(如健康與安全、環境、急難事件救援、技能與職業發展) 
- 如果數據以天為單位，則乘以 8，係假設 1天 = 8小時工作

每位員工教育訓練時數 平均每年每位員工總教育訓練時數  
資料來源： Refinitiv ，凱基

### Page 1

十月 29, 2026 研究網站: https://investment.kgisia.com.tw/Portal/Report/Index/Zh/R  Powered by the EFA Platform 1

動態更新 印刷電路板 ‧ 台灣

台燿 
(6274.TW /6274 TT)

ASIC AI 伺服器客戶群將進一步擴大 
重要訊息 
公司公布 3Q26 季報，毛利率及營利率 36.9%、31%，優共識 31.8%、
25.9%，淨利 38.6 億，優於共識 15%。 
評論及分析  
3Q26 毛利率及營利率表現亮眼，4Q26 高階產品開始調漲售價 。3Q26 高階
產品營收仍成長，但因中低階漲價幅度較大，導致 M7/M8 營收占比小幅下
降，加上泰國廠獲利 3Q26 轉盈，並彌補上半年虧損，使毛利率優預期。
M7/M8  (Networking 與 AI Server 占各半)、M4/M6  佔比分別季減 1、3 百分
點至 38%、28%；高壓材料 、Non-Low Loss，佔比季增 1、3 百分點至
15%、19%。4Q26 預期泰國廠仍可增加 10 萬張產能，而全系列 CCL 有漲
價機會，尤其高階 M7/M8 開始補漲，中低階因玻纖布供應緊張持續調漲、
泰國廠持續貢獻獲利， 有助改善產品組合與獲利。  
ASIC AI 伺服器正成為當前的核心成長引擎， 衛星與太空資料中心則開闢了
長期的發展藍海。摘要如下：  (1) AI Server 將 成為最大成長動能，新增數家
大型 ASIC 客戶，部分已進入送樣與板廠認證階段；而 美系 T3 伺服器持續有
供應商機，T4 預估 2Q27 有較明確規格及送樣進展。(2)  既有 400G Switch
客戶正加速認證 800G 及 1.6T Switch，部分客戶預期 2027 年 1.6T 需求可能
超越 800G，預期 Networking 客戶基礎較半年前顯著擴大。(3) 太空衛星板預
期 1H27 開始貢獻，材料 M8 以上。公司將逐步降低低階地面衛星板占比，
轉向高價值太空應用；客戶長期需求預測已延伸至 2032 年。(4) PTFE 公司提
出多種 Hybrid 解決方案，已有約 5-6 年研發基礎，並非新投入領域，但量產
設備仍需擴充，且受特殊高溫壓機產能限制，可能尋求外部合作。  
投資建議  
由於 3Q26 財報及 4Q26 展望優預期，我們上調 2027 獲利 34%，目標價上
調至 2070 元，相當於 2027 年每股盈餘 26 倍本益比 (先前為 32 倍)。 
投資風險  
客戶需求不如預期；競爭對手進度超前。  
增加持股‧維持

圖 4: 公司概況  圖 5: 產品應用別占比 
台燿公司成立於 1974 年 5 月，為國內四大銅箔基板供應商之
一，位於新竹縣竹北市， 2003 年 12 月在台掛牌上市。 2024
年產品組合為 HSD low loss 63% 、HSD non -low loss 23% 、
HDI 6% 及其他 9%。公司目前銅箔基板產能約 260 萬張，膠
片產能 4.5 萬捲(rolls)，壓合代工每月 155 萬平方英呎 (內層)。

營業比重(3Q26)

個股與大盤走勢(%)

許哲豪 
Jeff.syu@ctbcsis.com

面板及零組件 
友達 (2409 TT)

維持評等  
中立(Neutral) 
面板需求持續下滑，本業虧損擴大  
⚫ 三大業務獲利顯著衰退  
⚫ 營運展望承壓，面板需求持續 下滑  
⚫ 公司持續推動轉型，押注非面板前瞻技術  
3Q26毛利率下滑，受產品組合及消費性需求疲弱 ：公司於 2026年 10
月 30日舉行法說會，公告 3Q26財務數據，營收 669億元 (QoQ -5.7%，
YoY-4.3%)，毛利率 10.1%(QoQ -2.9ppts，YoY+0.5ppts)，營業淨利率
-3.5%(QoQ -3.8ppts，YoY-0.9ppts)，營業外利益 3.65 億元，單季 EPS 
-0.33元。3Q26  Display、Mobility 及 Vertical 業務營收占比分別為 46%、
30%、20%，主要衰退來自 Display 業務，因品牌廠上半年 提前拉貨結
束，且 Monitor/NB 持續受記憶體與 IC缺貨影響，需求持續放緩，客
戶拉貨持續持保守態度，Mobility Solutions 及 Vertical Solutions 業務
表現相對持穩。整體 營收規模下滑， 三大業務獲利表現均較前季顯著
下滑，且公司持續在車用及新技術開發投入研究，使營業費用高於預
期，本業虧損擴大。  
4Q26 面板需求持續下滑 ，預期 Display 業務仍衰退：公司指引 4Q26
三大業務， Mobility Solutions 呈季持平，雖車市受零組件短缺及中國
內需市場保守並轉趨出口導向影響歐洲車廠，但公司車用業務仍相對
穩健； Vertical Solutions 若未考慮能源事業 併表影響下，季減低個位
數百分比；Display 業務延續 IT產品需求疲弱，客戶採購保守，短期
內記憶體及零組件缺貨影響無法緩解，預期仍呈現營收下滑 。受面板
業務持續下滑影響，我們預期 4Q26營收 638億元 (QoQ -4.59%)，毛利
率 10.46%(QoQ+0.06ppts) 。 
推動轉型，友達成立創新研究院，聚焦 前瞻技術：友達組織結構調整，
新設創新研究院，將著重在 MicroLED、光通訊、玻璃基板、 AR光波
導及低軌衛星通訊天線 。在短中長距離傳輸應用上，友達開發的光引
擎可依客戶需求提供 MicroLED 或 Micro VCSEL 技術方案，目前已與
客戶進行規格討論，並建置小量研發線，預計量產投資在一到兩年後
技術成熟時大規模展開。 而在玻璃基板方面，公司在鍍銅、 RDL佈線
及 TGV皆有技術佈局， 玻璃尺寸包含 510x515mm、310x310mm 等規
格，並持續開發相關技術。  
公司在 Micro LED 光通訊應用及玻璃基板相關技術開發仍屬初期階
段，短期尚無實質營收貢獻， 我們預估 202 6-27年 EPS為-0.47、-0.34
元，公司過往 PBR區間約為 0.6x~1 .5x，我們基於 2027年預估之每股
淨值 x1.2倍 PBR，目標價為 22.4 元，維持中立之投資評等建議。 風
險因子：面板報價出貨不如預期 、消費性電子 需求不如預期 。

Document 667: 固本收利：聯準會升息了，投等債還能買嗎？

聯準會升息了，投等債還能買嗎？ 
 9 月聯準會升息一碼，為 2023 年 7 月以來首度升息，雖年內尚具升息空間，但
資金流仍持續流入美國投等債，顯示市況紛擾無損其資金避風港的特質。

出刊日：2026/10/8 
222 期

### Page 2

2026

02-8751-6665 
 www.fubon.com 
台北市大安區仁愛路四段 169 號 
固本收利

本期內容： 
⚫ 經濟數據、市場供
需等持續干擾債市 
⚫ 美國投等債仍為資
金良好避風港 
⚫ 建議逢利率回彈時
分批佈局投等債

重點摘要

■ 為因應頑固的通膨壓力 ，9 月聯準會升
息 1 碼，預期年內至少再升息 1 碼。
第 3 季美國投等債價格承壓是受美國
數據強勁、大量投等債發債、通膨預期
加上聯準會鷹派訊號等因素影響。

■ 總體經濟強韌提供絕佳的信用支撐，
高評級公司違約風險或信用降評機率
微乎其微。今年以來美國投資等級債
維持淨流入， 顯示市場動盪時美國投
等債仍為資金避風港。

■ 目前美國投等債殖利率 處於近年相對
較高的位置，投資人應以息收為主要
考量，建議投資人把握殖利率回彈時
機，分批進行佈局鎖定高債息收益。

圖一：美國投等債殖利率達近年相對高位

▋ 聯準會 3 年多來首度升息 
9 月聯準會票委全數贊成通過升息 1 碼，將
聯邦資金利率目標調升為 3.75%至 4.00%
區間，為 2023 年 7 月以來首度升息。美國
經濟維持穩健擴張 ，聯準會對於 2026、
2027 年經濟成長預期均上修 0.1 個百分點
至 2.3%、2.4%，就業供需平穩且失業率穩
定，2026、2027 年失業率預期均下修 0.2
個百分點至 4.1%，會後聲明新增國內支出
具韌性描述。今年 PCE 和核心 PCE 均上修
0.1 個百分點至 3.7%和 3.4%，9 月聯準會
升息是為因應頑固的通膨壓力，18 名與會
官員中有 16 位委員預期今年內至少再升息
1 碼，明年聯準會維持 6 月的通膨預期， 政
策利率亦維持不變。(圖二、圖三)

圖二：美國經濟穩健，通膨具僵固性 
預測數據變動 9 月更新 6 月預測

▋ 殖利率飆壓抑 Q3 投等債價格 
第 3 季美國投等債價格下跌，是今年最差
單季表現 ， 相比去年第3 季以及過去 30 年
第 3 季的投等債價格表現亦相對遜色。這
是受到近期美國就業 、 生產數據表現強勁 ，
今年前 8 個月科技相關投等債發債量已超
越去年全年表現 ， 中東戰爭持續支撐國際油
價在高位 ， 而居高不下的通膨預期持續推升
美債殖利率走勢等 ， 加上聯準會主席Warsh
自 6 月主持個人首場利率會議起，即反覆
強調聯準會穩定物價的鷹派訊號， 持續為投
等債走勢構成壓力。(圖四、圖五)

圖四：科技相關投資等級債發債量看增

▋ 那投資等級債還能買嗎？ 
美國經濟數據強勁意味在強韌的總體經濟
下 ， 提供絕佳的信用支撐 ， 即便面臨高利率
環境 ， 大型龍頭企業營運有望維持穩健 ， 加
上企業帳上現金充裕 ， 投資等級高的公司違
約風險或信用降評的機率將微乎其微。

隨著各國債券殖利率攀升 ，加劇全球風險資
產高檔震盪 ， 市場避險情緒明顯升溫， 今年
以來美國投資等級債維持淨流入， 根據小摩
數據顯示，至 9/24 流入投等債資金已直逼
2025 年，相比非投等債和新興市場債僅達
去年約一半的水準，顯示在市場動盪時 ， 美
國投資等級債的高流動性和 極佳避險韌性
仍為資金提供避風港而持續吸金。(圖六)

圖六：目前流入投等債資金直逼去年水準 
資料來源：JPM，2026/9/24

▋ 殖利率達近年高點具吸引力 
目前美國投等債利差落在近年低點 ， 相對較
無收斂空間 ， 但殖利率則落在5%至 6%間，
處於近年相對較高的位置 ， 高殖利具有投資
吸引力 ， 投資人應以息收為主要考量 ， 目前
包含金融公司、通訊產業債殖利率在6%以
上 ， 為殖利率相對領先的產業族群 ， 建議在
當前升息預期濃厚下 ， 投資人應把握殖利率
回彈的時機 ， 分批進行佈局以鎖定高債息收
益。(圖七、圖八)

圖七：目前投等債以息收為主 
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/9/24

圖八：目前金融 、 科技等投等債殖利率較高 
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/9/24

3Q26 獲利優於預期， 4Q26 可望擺脫過往淡季  
⚫ 3Q26毛利率略優於市場預期 ，4Q26有望再季增  
⚫ B/B ratio 仍維持 2.0，AI應用佔比持續擴張  
⚫ 產能利用率維持滿載，預期 2Q27仍有新產能開出  
3Q26 營收獲利符合預期， 4Q26 成長動能延續 ：國巨  3Q26 營收達
495.31 億元，季增 11.4%，年增 49.7%，符合我們與市場預期，主要
受惠 AI 需求持續升溫，帶動各產品線及區域營收同步成長。毛利率
達 41%，季增 2.5個百分點，略優於市場預期 1~2個百分點，主要受
惠價格調整效益及高毛利 AI 產品占比提升 ，EPS 為 5.83 元。展望
4Q26，考量 AI需求延續、特殊品接單動能優於標準品，加上價格調
整效益與產品組合持續優化，預期營收及獲利率將同步季增，淡季表
現可望優於往年，我們預估 4Q26營收將季增約 5.18 %至 521億元。 
B/B ratio 維持 2.0高檔， AI應用佔比擴至 20%：國巨 3Q26接單動能
維持強勁，整體 B/B ratio 接近 2.0，其中 AI相關鉭質電容與 MLCC
需求最為旺盛，部分產品線更超過 2.0。儘管 B/B ratio 較前季略降，
主因營收成長帶動出貨增加，並非接單動能轉弱。 AI已成為主要成長
動能，營收占比由 1Q26 的 15%提升至 3Q26 約 20%，且成長趨勢逐
步擴及電阻、電感及 電源相關電容。供給方面， 3Q26標準品與特殊品
產能利用率均約 95%，管理層預期 4Q26將進一步提升。  
產能利用率逼近滿載， 2Q27新產能開出 : 在產能利用率已達高檔下，
國巨規劃擴充鉭質電容與 MLCC 產能，預計總產能增加 10%~15%，
但新產能須至 2Q27起才分階段開出，短期供給彈性有限。通路方面，
全球經銷商庫存已由約 5個月降至 4個月，大中華區更低於 2個月，
後續仍有補庫存需求。在 AI 需求成長與供給擴張存在時間差的情況
下，國巨短期有望受惠供需緊俏帶來的報價調整機會，後續獲利成長
則取決於漲價落實程度及 AI高附加價值產品比重提升幅度。  
考量國際主要被動元件大廠本益比已回落至約 25 倍，市場對產業成
長的評價趨於審慎，國巨原先 35 倍的成長溢價已較難維持。因此，
本次將目標本益比由 35倍下修至 25倍，使評價回歸國際同業水準，
並以 2027年 EPS為基礎，目標價為 841元，建議買進。 
風險因子 : AI需求放緩、競爭者加速擴產 。

日韓擴產難解高階缺口，國巨有望迎來一般品漲價與產品組合優化紅利 : 從三星
電機 10/29 法說會及村田製作所的投資動向來看， AI伺服器對高容值 MLCC 的
需求快速攀升，三星預估部分產品 2027年需求將達 2026年的 8倍；村田 10月
中表示原定投入的 800億日圓擴產計畫亦已不足以滿足需求，目前正評估追加投
資。由於日韓大廠持續將資源集中於高階產品，一般品供給彈性可能受到壓縮 ，
有助於改善部分產品的供需結構，並帶動報價回升 。對國巨而言，受惠不僅來自
AI 相關鉭質電容、電阻及電感等產品的需求成長，也包括一般品報價調整的潛
在機會。在產能利用率已處高檔的情況下，若漲價效益逐步反映於出貨，加上高
附加價值產品比重提升，將有助於推升毛利率與獲利；惟目前消費性電子終端需
求尚未明顯復甦，後續仍須觀察轉單規模、一般品實際漲價幅度，以及日韓大廠
產能調整對市場供給的實質影響。

Document 804: 美国经济增长超预期，私人投资与服务消费成为主要支撑

Source PDF: all report pdf/美国经济增长超预期，私人投资与服务消费成为主要支撑.pdf
Report source: 联储证券
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 16
Status: ok

### Page 1

证券研究报告 
宏观经济点评 2026 年 11 月 04 日

请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明

美国经济增长超预期，私人投资与服务消费成为
主要支撑

### Page 2

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 2 
点，改善主要来自批发贸易和制造业，且均以非耐用品为主要驱动因素。
从美国人口普查局数据看，7—8 月月均销售额为 6586.4 亿美元，高于去
年同期的 6076.9 亿美元；同期库存销售比为 1.34 和 1.33，低于去年同期
的 1.40。销售增长较强、库存销售比下降，意味着本季度库存增加或更多
是企业在终端需求保持韧性的背景下主动补库。后续仍需关注销售增长能
否持续，以及库存销售比是否趋势性回升。 
消费保持稳定，服务消费支撑增强，居民信贷供给值得关注。 个人消费支
出（PCE）对实际 GDP 环比折年率的贡献为 2.52 个百分点，与二季度的
2.51 个百分点基本持平，但内部结构有所变化：服务消费贡献上升，商品
消费贡献下降。商品消费贡献由二季度的 0.94 个百分点降至 0.63 个百分
点。增速方面，三季度商品消费环比折年率增长 3.0%，低于二季度的
4.5%，但仍处于相对高位；其中耐用品消费增速由 7.4%降至 2.3%，非
耐用品消费则由 3.1%小幅升至 3.4%。耐用品消费贡献下降，主要受到机
动车及零部件、家具及耐用家居设备消费走弱的拖累；非耐用品消费贡献
则有所上升，主要来自外出购买的食品饮料以及服装鞋类消费。服务消费
贡献由 1.57 个百分点升至 1.89 个百分点，成为消费增长的主要支撑。 服
务消费增速也由二季度的 3.4%升至 4.1%，为 2024 年以来最高。分项来
看，医疗保健、金融服务及保险的贡献较二季度有所回落，但医疗保健仍
是贡献最大的服务分项，达到 0.56 个百分点。与此同时，住房及公用事
业、餐饮住宿消费贡献上升，带动家庭服务消费贡献达到 1.76 个百分
点，虽较去年同期高点略低，但仍处于较高水平。收入与信贷方面，居民
收入有所修复，信贷扩张或为消费提供额外支撑。 个人可支配收入
（DPI）增速在 2025 年筑底后，2026 年以来小幅反弹，三季度达到
4.8%。与此同时，根据纽约联储的调查，信用卡授信额度（CC Limit）仍
处于较快上行阶段，而信用卡余额（CC Balance）增速明显低于授信额
度增速；房屋净值信贷额度（HELOC Limit）及余额（HELOC Balance）
也呈上升趋势。整体来看，居民收入改善叠加信贷空间扩张，或为消费提
供一定支撑，但后续消费能否维持较高增速，仍取决于收入增长能否持
续，以及居民能否继续获得银行信贷支持。 
三季度政府支出由拖累转为拉动，主要受联邦非国防支出回升带动 。政府
支出对实际 GDP 环比折年率的贡献由二季度的-0.01 个百分点升至三季度
的 0.90 个百分点，增长全部来自联邦政府支出， 联邦政府和州及地方政
府分别贡献 0.91 和-0.01 个百分点。其中，联邦非国防支出改善最为明
显，贡献由二季度的-0.34 个百分点升至三季度的 0.49 个百分点。其增速
也由二季度的-12.7%大幅反弹至 22.2%，是联邦政府支出回升的重要来
源。这一变化可能与战略石油储备（SPR）原油投放规模收窄有关。 三季
度 SPR 库存下降 3572.2 万桶，明显少于二季度的 8767 万桶，期末库存
降至 2.83 亿桶，创 1982 年以来新低1。根据 BEA 的核算规则，政府出售
SPR 原油会冲减政府消费支出，出售的原油则转入私人部门核算，交易本
身不直接增加 GDP。因此，三季度 SPR 投放规模低于二季度，可能减轻
了对联邦政府消费支出的核算拖累。从美国国内原油供给看，SPR 库存

### Page 3

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 3 
已处于低位，进一步释放储备的空间或相对有限；商业原油库存则是私人
部门调节供需的重要缓冲，其变化将影响美国国内原油供给的稳定性。
2026 年三季度美国炼油厂平均开工率达到 95%，原油加工量创 2019 年
以来同期新高，反映国内炼油需求较为旺盛2。这说明在战略储备投放收
窄的情况下，美国商业炼油活动仍保持较高水平，但后续商业库存能否提
供足够缓冲，仍需结合库存和进出口数据观察。从全球供给看，中东原油
供给能否恢复，将成为影响后续原油市场平衡的重要变量。 EIA 预计，随
着管道及陆路绕行、船对船转运，以及阿联酋计划于 2027 年投运的新绕
行管道等替代出口方案逐步落地，中东地区原油停产规模有望在 短期出口
预测期内逐步下降。EIA 数据显示，7 个主要产油国 3—7 月平均停产规模
约为 851.2 万桶/日，9 月已降至 477.0 万桶/日，并预计 2026 年四季度和
2027 年一季度进一步降至 447.0 万桶/日和 268.3 万桶/日3。后续来看，
若美国商业原油供给保持稳定、中东原油供给逐步恢复， SPR 投放规模
或进一步收窄，其对政府支出分项的核算拖累也可能持续减弱。 但仍需关
注中东局势演变、中东原油停产规模能否如 EIA 预期逐步回落及美国商业
原油供给变化。 
净出口对经济增长的拖累幅度明显扩大。三季度商品和服务净出口对实际
GDP 环比折年率的贡献为-3.32 个百分点，为 2025 年二季度以来最大拖
累。其中，出口和进口分别拖累 0.68 和 2.64 个百分点，进口增长是主要
拖累来源。从增速看，三季度出口环比折年率下降 5.7%，由二季度的增
长 5.0%转为负增长；其中商品出口下降 7.4%，服务出口下降 2.6%。进
口则增长 19.7%，较二季度的 12.6%进一步加快；其中商品进口增长
26.4%，高于二季度的 15.3%，服务进口则下降 4.2%。分项来看，出口
下降主要受商品出口减少拖累，其中石油及相关产品是主要拖累项；进口
则明显增长，主要拉动力来自汽车以外的资本品，或反映企业资本品采购
需求较强。 
美国经济增长动能回升，内需保持韧性，后续增长持续性仍需关注。 美国
三季度实际 GDP 主要受私人投资回升、消费保持韧性及联邦政府支出增
加带动。其中，设备投资和库存补充贡献较大，消费则呈现商品放缓、服
务走强的结构性变化。与此同时，进口大幅增长、出口转负，净出口成为
主要拖累。展望后续，美国经济增长仍有私人需求支撑，但部分阶段性增
长动力的持续性有待观察。一方面，AI 相关资本开支有望继续支撑设备投
资，但需关注投资能否转化为生产率提升；同时，终端需求与企业补库意
愿能否延续，也将影响库存投资对经济增长的支撑力度 ；另一方面，服务
消费或在居民收入和信贷支撑下保持韧性，但就业、收入及信贷状况的边
际变化可能影响居民消费的持续性。此外，资本品进口需求变化及中东原
油供给恢复进度，也可能影响净出口和政府支出的后续表现。

风险提示：地缘冲突升级风险；美联储政策超预期收紧风险；AI 投资推升

### Page 7

宏观经济点评
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 7
1. 美国经济增长超预期，私人投资与服务消费成为主要支
撑
1.1 美国经济增长超预期，内需保持韧性，货币政策预期仍存分歧
美国 2026 年三季度实际 GDP 环比折年率增长 3.4%， 高于市场预期的3.2%， 较二
季度的 2.2%明显回升， 反映美国经济增长仍具韧性。其中，私人部门需求保持强劲。实
际私人国内最终销售（Real Final Sales to Private Domestic Purchasers，即消费支出与
私人固定投资之和）增长 4.9%，高于二季度的 4.6%，表明消费和私人投资仍为经济增
长提供支撑。 与此同时，国内购买价格指数涨幅有所回落， 三季度上涨3.0%， 低于二季
度的 5.6%，显示国内购买价格压力有所缓和。 数据公布后，CME 利率期货定价显示，
市场对 2026 年 12 月加息一次的预期仍较强，概率为 71.4%。不过，市场对 2027 年加
息幅度的预期有所下调， 由此前定价的加息两次降至一次。 进一步看， 在2026 年 12 月

加息一次的情景下， 截至2027 年 12 月， 维持利率不变、 再加息一次和再加息两次的概
率分别为 22.8%、34.7%和 25.0%，各情景概率仍较为接近，表明市场对后续利率路径
的判断尚未收敛，仍存在较大不确定性。
1.2 私人投资明显回升，设备投资与库存补充共同支撑增 长
私人投资对实际 GDP 环比折年率的贡献升至 3.27 个百分点， 明显高于前值0.82 个
百分点及去年同期的 0.15 个百分点。 其中， 私人固定投资和私人库存变动分别贡献1.66
和 1.61 个百分点， 二者对本季度私人投资回升的贡献较为接近。从增速看， 三季度私人
投资环比折年率增长 19.4%，明显高于二季度的 4.6%；其中固定投资增长 9.4%，高于
二季度的 7.7%。固定投资方面，设备投资是主要拉动力。 非住宅固定投资今年以来对
GDP 增长的贡献均在 1 个百分点以上，三季度达到 1.78 个百分点。其中，设备投资贡
献 1.16 个百分点， 主要受计算机及外围设备投资加速带动， 该分项贡献由二季度的-0.09

个百分点升至 0.72 个百分点。设备投资环比折年率增长 20.9%，较二季度的 13.4%进
一步加快， 显示企业设备采购需求较强。知识产权产品投资贡献小幅回落至 0.47 个百分
点，增速由二季度的 9.2%降至 8.4%；Structures 在连续五个季度贡献下降或持平后，
本季度转正， 但回升能否持续仍有待观察。 相比之下， 住宅投资再度转负， 拖累GDP 增
长 0.12 个百分点， 反映利率敏感型投资或仍相对疲弱。库存方面， 批发贸易和制造业库
存变动是主要拉动力。私人库存变动对 GDP 增长的贡献由二季度的-0.53 个百分点升至
1.61 个百分点，改善主要来自批发贸易和制造业，且均以非耐用品为主要驱动因素。从
美国人口普查局数据看，7—8 月月均销售额为 6586.4 亿美元，高于去年同期的 6076.9
亿美元；同期库存销售比为 1.34 和 1.33，低于去年同期的 1.40。销售增长较强、库存
销售比下降，意味着本季度库存增加或更多是企业在终端需求保持韧性的背景下主动补

库。后续仍需关注销售增长能否持续，以及库存销售比是否趋势性回升。
1.3 消费保持稳定，服务消费支撑增强，居民信贷供给值得关注
个人消费支出（PCE）对实际 GDP 环比折年率的贡献为 2.52 个百分点，与二季度
的 2.51 个百分点基本持平， 但内部结构有所变化：服务消费贡献上升， 商品消费贡献下
降。商品消费贡献由二季度的 0.94 个百分点降至 0.63 个百分点。增速方面，三季度商
品消费环比折年率增长 3.0%，低于二季度的 4.5%，但仍处于相对高位；其中耐用品消
费增速由 7.4%降至 2.3%，非耐用品消费则由 3.1%小幅升至 3.4%。耐用品消费贡献下
降，主要受到机动车及零部件、家具及耐用家居设备消费走弱的拖累；非耐用品消费贡
献则有所上升，主要来自外出购买的食品饮料以及服装鞋类消费。服务消费贡献由 1.57
个百分点升至 1.89 个百分点，成为消费增长的主要支撑。 服务消费增速也由二季度的
3.4%升至 4.1%，为 2024 年以来最高。分项来看， 医疗保健、 金融服务及保险的贡献较

### Page 8

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 8 
贷扩张或为消费提供额外支撑。 个人可支配收入（DPI）增速在 2025 年筑底后，2026
年以来小幅反弹， 三季度达到4.8%。 与此同时，根据纽约联储的调查，信用卡授信额度
（CC Limit）仍处于较快上行阶段，而信用卡余额（CC Balance）增速明显低于授信额
度增速； 房屋净值信贷额度 （HELOC Limit） 及余额 （HELOC Balance） 也呈上升趋势。
整体来看，居民收入改善叠加信贷空间扩张，或为消费提供一定支撑，但后续消费能否
维持较高增速，仍取决于收入增长能否持续，以及居民能否继续获得银行信贷支持。  
1.4 政府支出由拖累转为拉动，主要受联邦非国防支出回升带动 
政府支出对实际 GDP 环比折年率的贡献由二季度的-0.01 个百分点升至三季度的
0.90 个百分点，增长全部来自联邦政府支出，联邦政府和州及地方政府分别贡献 0.91 和
-0.01 个百分点。其中，联邦非国防支出改善最为明显，贡献由二季度的-0.34 个百分点
升至三季度的 0.49 个百分点。其增速也由二季度的-12.7%大幅反弹至 22.2%，是联邦
政府支出回升的重要来源。这一变化可能与战略石油储备（SPR）原油投放规模收窄有
关。三季度 SPR 库存下降 3572.2 万桶，明显少于二季度的 8767 万桶，期末库存降至
2.83 亿桶， 创1982 年以来新低4。 根据BEA 的核算规则， 政府出售SPR 原油会冲减政
府消费支出， 出售的原油则转入私人部门核算， 交易本身不直接增加GDP。 因此，三季
度 SPR 投放规模低于二季度， 可能减轻了对联邦政府消费支出的核算拖累。从美国国内
原油供给看，SPR 库存已处于低位， 进一步释放储备的空间或相对有限； 商业原油库存
则是私人部门调节供需的重要缓冲， 其变化将影响美国国内原油供给的稳定性。2026 年
三季度美国炼油厂平均开工率达到 95%，原油加工量创 2019 年以来同期新高，反映国
内炼油需求较为旺盛5。 这说明在战略储备投放收窄的情况下， 美国商业炼油活动仍保持
较高水平，但后续商业库存能否提供足够缓冲，仍 需结合库存和进出口数据观察。从全
球供给看，中东原油供给能否恢复，将成为影响后续原油市场平衡的重要变量。 EIA 预
计，随着管道及陆路绕行、船对船转运，以及阿联酋计划于 2027 年投运的新绕行管道
等替代出口方案逐步落地，中东地区原油停产规模有望在 短期出口预测期内逐步下降。
EIA 数据显示，7 个主要产油国 3—7 月平均停产规模约为 851.2 万桶/日，9 月已降至
477.0 万桶/日，并预计 2026 年四季度和 2027 年一季度进一步降至 447.0 万桶/日和
268.3 万桶/日6。后续来看，若美国商业原油供给保持稳定、中东原油供给 逐步恢复，
SPR 投放规模或进一步收窄， 其对政府支出分项的核算拖累也可能持续减弱。但仍需关
注中东局势演变、 中东原油停产规模能否如 EIA 预期逐步回落及美国商业原油供给变化。 
1.5 净出口对经济增长的拖累幅度明显扩大 
三季度商品和服务净出口对实际 GDP 环比折年率的贡献为-3.32 个百分点， 为2025
年二季度以来最大拖累。其中，出口和进口分别拖累 0.68 和 2.64 个百分点，进口增长
是主要拖累来源。从增速看，三季度出口环比折年率下降 5.7%，由二季度的增长 5.0%
转为负增长；其中商品出口下降 7.4%，服务出口下降 2.6%。进口则增长 19.7%，较二
季度的 12.6%进一步加快；其中商品进口增长 26.4%，高于二季度的 15.3%，服务进口
则下降 4.2%。分项来看， 出口下降主要受商品出口减少拖累， 其中石油及相关产品是主
要拖累项；进口则明显增长，主要拉动力来自汽车以外的资本品，或反映企业资本品采
购需求较强。 
1.6 美国经济增长动能回升，内需保持韧性，后续增长持续性仍需关注 
美国三季度实际 GDP 主要受私人投资回升、消费保持韧性及联邦政府支出增加带
动。其中，设备投资和库存补充贡献较大，消费则呈现商品放缓、服务走强的结构 性变

### Page 9

宏观经济点评  
请务必阅读正文之后的信息披露和法律声明 9 
化。与此同时，进口大幅增长、出口转负，净出口成为主要拖累。 展望后续，美国经济
增长仍有私人需求支撑，但部分阶段性增长动力的持续性有待观察。一方面，AI 相关资
本开支有望继续支撑设备投资，但需关注投资能否转化为生产率提升；同时，终端需求
与企业补库意愿能否延续，也将影响库存投资对经济增长的支撑力度 ；另一方面，服务
消费或在居民收入和信贷支撑下保持韧性，但就业、收入及信贷状况的边际变化可能影
响居民消费的持续性。此外，资本品进口需求变化及中东原油供给恢复进度， 也可能影
响净出口和政府支出的后续表现。 
2. 风险提示 
地缘冲突升级风险；美联储政策超预期收紧风险；AI 投资推升成本风险。
